\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.84 0 L 49.21 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 49.21 0 L 72.83 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 78.74 0 M 84.65 0 C 84.65 3.26 82 5.91 78.74 5.91 C 75.48 5.91 72.83 3.26 72.83 0 C 72.83 -3.26 75.48 -5.91 78.74 -5.91 C 82 -5.91 84.65 -3.26 84.65 0 Z M 78.74 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 74.02 4.72 L 83.46 -4.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 74.02 -4.72 L 83.46 4.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

\displaystyle=\>\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{\phi}^{2}}{3!}\sum_{a,b}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,(ab)\,\varphi^{a}(x)\left(\Delta_{\phi}^{ab}(x,x^{\prime})\right)^{3}\varphi^{b}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{i}g^{2}}{2}\sum_{a,b}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}(ab)\,\varphi^{a}(x)\left(\Delta_{\chi}^{ab}(x,x^{\prime})\right)^{2}\Delta_{\phi}^{ab}(x,x^{\prime})\varphi^{b}(x^{\prime})\,,

(21)

where in the first line we suppressed symmetry factors. Above, a dashed line denotes the
\phi
propagator
\Delta_{\phi}
, a solid line the
\chi
propagator
\Delta_{\chi}
, and a dashed line ending with a crossed circle indicates an insertion of the background
\varphi
at the corresponding vertex. Taking the variation of Eq. (
21
) with respect to the condensate, we find the two-loop retarded self-energy entering the condensate EoM (
20
). It reads

\displaystyle\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(x,x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{\phi}^{2}}{3!}\left[(\Delta_{\phi}^{++}(x,x^{\prime}))^{3}-(\Delta_{\phi}^{+-}(x,x^{\prime}))^{3}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}g^{2}}{2}\left[\Delta_{\phi}^{++}(x,x^{\prime})(\Delta_{\chi}^{++}(x,x^{\prime}))^{2}-\Delta_{\phi}^{+-}(x,x^{\prime})(\Delta_{\chi}^{+-}(x,x^{\prime}))^{2}\right]\,,

(22)

as follows from Eqs. (
17
), (
21
) and Eq. (
11
).
Dissipative processes described by the diagrams in Eq. (
21
) can be interpreted via the cutting rules at finite temperature
[
112
,
113
,
114
,
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
]
and for bubble wall dynamics these are scattering processes between particles and the background field, i.e. the bubble. For an oscillating condensate, they can also describe its decay
[
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
107
]
.

A distinct feature of these processes is that they involve the condensate-particle vertices. In our toy model, these vertices are
\varphi\phi^{3}
and
\varphi\phi\chi^{2}
. In the conventional bubble EoM (
1a
), the bubble wall interacts with particles only through the modified dispersion relation of the fluctuation fields, i.e. via
\varphi
-dependent mass terms of the fluctuation fields. The condensate-particle vertices also give rise to new collision terms in the Boltzmann equations and could also be important there.

Equation (
20
) is non-local and consequently it is extremely difficult to solve exactly and analytically. The standard procedure for localisation is to do the Wigner transform for the correlation functions and then perform the gradient expansion. A detailed discussion of this procedure can be found in, e.g., Ref.
[
119
]
(See also
[
107
]
for a pedagogical introduction).

The Wigner transform of a two-point function
G(x_{1},x_{2})
is defined as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(k,x)=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}r\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\cdot r}G\left(x+\frac{r}{2},x-\frac{r}{2}\right)\,,

(23)

where
x=(x_{1}+x_{2})/2
and
r=x_{1}-x_{2}
. The inverse Wigner transform reads

\displaystyle G(x,x^{\prime})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k(x-x^{\prime})}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\left(k,\frac{x+x^{\prime}}{2}\right)\,.

(24)

In the quasi-particle limit and at leading order in the gradient expansion, the propagators in Wigner space
\{k,x\}
read
[
120
]
9
9

9

Note the difference between our notation and that in Ref.
[
120
]
; Our
\Delta
is the
\mathrm{i}\Delta
of Ref.
[
120
]

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{<}(k,x)

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})(1+f(-\mathbf{k},x))\right]\,,

(25a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{>}(k,x)

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})(1+f(\mathbf{k},x))+\vartheta(-k^{0})f(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(25b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{T}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\rm i}{k^{2}-m^{2}+{\rm i}\varepsilon}+2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})f(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(25c)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\bar{T}}(k,x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\rm i}{k^{2}-m^{2}-{\rm i}\varepsilon}+2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})f(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(25d)

where the particle density
f
is a function of the space-time coordinate
x
, the
three
-momentum
\mathbf{k}
and
\vartheta
designates the Heaviside theta-function.
Note that in these expressions only the tree-level,
\varphi
-dependent mass
m^{2}
appears. Our procedure, however, can be easily generalised to the case when we include corrections to the mass by replacing
m^{2}\to M^{2}
, where
M^{2}
now receives both thermal and quantum corrections.

Fourier transforming Eq. (
25c
), one can write

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\phi}}{2}\varphi(x)\Delta^{++}_{\phi}(x,x)\equiv\frac{\partial\delta V_{\phi}(\varphi)}{\partial\varphi}+\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{\phi}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},\phi}\,{f_{\phi}(\mathbf{k},x)}\,,

(26a)

\displaystyle\frac{g}{2}\varphi(x)\Delta^{++}_{\chi}(x,x)\equiv\frac{\partial\delta V_{\chi}(\varphi)}{\partial\varphi}+\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{\chi}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},\chi}\,{f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k},x)}\,,

(26b)

where
E_{\phi/\chi}=\sqrt{m_{\phi/\chi}^{2}+\mathbf{k}^{2}}
. In Eq. (
26
) we introduced the following short-hand notation for phase space integrals with the Lorentz invariant measure for particle species
i

\int_{\mathbf{k},i}=\>\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}2E_{i}}\,,

(27)

which we will use in the remainder of this work. Wherever there is no room for confusion, we will drop the index
i
identifying the particle species.
We have identified the divergent zero-temperature one-loop correction to the classical potential

\frac{\partial\delta V_{\phi}(\varphi)}{\partial\varphi}=\>\frac{\lambda_{\phi}}{2}\varphi(x)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{k^{2}-m_{\phi}^{2}+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon}\,,

(28)

and analogously for
\chi
. These divergent corrections will be made finite following standard regularisation and renormalisation procedures and consequently, below, we should understand both
V_{0}
and
\delta V
as renormalised potentials.
Summing up, we find the zero-temperature one-loop effective potential

\displaystyle V_{0}(\varphi)+\delta V_{\phi}(\varphi)+\delta V_{\chi}(\varphi)=\>V_{0}(\varphi)+\delta V_{T=0}(\varphi)=\>V(\varphi)\,.

(29)

Then, we can write the condensate EoM as

\displaystyle\Box\varphi+V^{\prime}(\varphi)+\sum_{i=\phi,\chi}\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{i}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,{f_{i}(\mathbf{k},x)}+\hskip-48.36958pt\underbrace{\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(x,x^{\prime})\varphi(x^{\prime})}_{\rm overlooked\ in\ earlier\ studies\ on\ the\ kinetic\ approach}\hskip-42.67912pt=0\,.

(30)

We take
i=\phi,\chi
for the specific model we are considering. In general models,
i
runs over every field that obtains a
\varphi
-dependent mass term. Above, we have highlighted that the last term has been overlooked in the literature on bubble wall dynamics.

To understand how friction on the bubble wall enters the EoM, we consider a stationary wall and work in its rest frame. If the bubble radius is large compared to other length scales involved in the process, we can approximate the bubble to a planar wall.
We then assume that the wall moves in the
z
-direction, as shown in Fig.
1
. In this frame, due to the translation invariance in the time and in the spatial directions tangential to the wall, denoted as
\mathbf{x}_{\perp}
, it is convenient to define

\displaystyle\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})=\int\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\perp}\,\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(t-t^{\prime},\mathbf{x}_{\perp}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\perp};z,z^{\prime})\,,

(31)

so that

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(x,x^{\prime})\varphi(x^{\prime})=\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})\varphi(z^{\prime})\,.

(32)

Multiplying Eq. (
30
) by
\mathrm{d}\varphi/\mathrm{d}z
and integrating over
z
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}z}\left[\Box\varphi+V^{\prime}(\varphi)+\sum_{i=\phi,\chi}\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{i}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)+\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})\varphi(z^{\prime})\right]=0\,,

(33)

where
\delta
is a length scale larger than the wall width but still much smaller than the bubble radius,
L_{w}<\delta\ll R_{w}
.
The first term is zero because it is the integral of a total derivative of a quantity that vanishes for large
|z|
where the scalar field is constant.
The second term is also the integral of a total derivative, that, however, does not vanish at the boundaries. It yields the
zero-temperature
effective potential difference between the two phases,
\Delta V\equiv V(\delta)-V(-\delta)
. Finally, we find

\displaystyle\Delta V=-\sum_{i=\phi,\chi}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\mathrm{d}m_{i}^{2}(\varphi(z))}{\mathrm{d}z}\,\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}z}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})\varphi(z^{\prime})\,.

(34)

We identify the driving and the frictional pressures

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm driving}=\,\Delta V\,,

(35a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm friction}=\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}+\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}\,.

(35b)

We distinguish between the friction induced by the condensate-dependent mass term

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}\equiv\hskip-5.69046pt\underbrace{-\sum_{i}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\mathrm{d}m_{i}^{2}(\varphi(z))}{\mathrm{d}z}\,\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)}_{\mathrm{{included\ in\ earlier\ studies\ on\ the\ kinetic\ approach}}}\,\hskip-8.5359pt,

(36)

and the friction induced by condensate-dependent interaction vertices

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}\equiv\hskip-14.22636pt\underbrace{-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}z}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})\varphi(z^{\prime})}_{\mathrm{overlooked\ in\ {earlier\ studies\ of\ the\ kinetic\ approach}}}\,\hskip-19.91684pt.

(37)

3.2
Friction induced by condensate-dependent masses: process (1)

Let us first focus on the pressure induced by the
\varphi
-dependent mass term, Eq. (
36
). Here we discuss two typical cases: near local thermal equilibrium and the ultrarelativistic regime.

Near local thermal equilibrium.

Close to equilibrium, we expand the density functions as
{f_{i}=f_{i}^{\rm eq}+\delta f_{i}}
, so that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\phi,\chi}\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{i}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)=\frac{\partial\delta V_{T\neq 0}(\varphi)}{\partial\varphi}+\sum_{i=\phi,\chi}\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{i}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,\delta f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)\,.

(38)

Above, we have introduced the thermal correction to the potential
10
10

10

Identifying the RHS as the derivative of a potential correction with respect to
\varphi
is only valid at the leading-order in the derivative expansion due to
\varphi
-dependent masses. See Ref.
[
121
]
for a discussion beyond the derivative expansion.

\frac{\partial\delta V_{T\neq 0}(\varphi)}{\partial\varphi}=\>\sum_{i=\phi,\chi}\frac{\mathrm{d}m^{2}_{i}(\varphi)}{\mathrm{d}\varphi}\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,f_{i}^{\rm eq}(\mathbf{k},z)\,.

(39)

Combining
\delta V_{T\neq 0}
with
V
defines the effective potential
V_{\rm eff}
at finite temperature,

\displaystyle V_{\rm eff}(\varphi,T)\equiv V(\varphi)+\delta V_{T\neq 0}(\varphi,T)\,.

(40)

Then Eq. (
36
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}z}\frac{\partial\delta V_{T\neq 0}(\varphi,T)}{\partial\varphi}-\sum_{i}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\mathrm{d}m_{i}^{2}(\varphi(z))}{\mathrm{d}z}\,\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,\delta f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\delta V_{T\neq 0}}{\mathrm{d}z}-\frac{\partial\delta V_{T\neq 0}(\varphi,T)}{\partial T}\frac{\mathrm{d}T}{\mathrm{d}z}\right)-\sum_{i}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\mathrm{d}m_{i}^{2}(\varphi(z))}{\mathrm{d}z}\,\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\delta f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)

\displaystyle=-\Delta\delta V_{T\neq 0}+\mathcal{P}_{\rm LTE}+\mathcal{P}_{\rm dissipative}\,,

(41)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm LTE}=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\partial\delta V_{T\neq 0}}{\partial T}\frac{\mathrm{d}T}{\mathrm{d}z}\,,

(42a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm dissipative}=\underbrace{-\sum_{i}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\frac{\mathrm{d}m_{i}^{2}(\varphi(z))}{\mathrm{d}z}\,\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\delta f_{i}(\mathbf{k},z)}_{\text{out-of-equilibrium\ effects}}\,.

(42b)

\mathcal{P}_{\rm LTE}
is the friction that persists in local thermal equilibrium and is due to the inhomogeneous temperature distribution of the plasma across the wall
[
67
]
.

In Ref.
[
67
]
,
\Delta\delta V_{T\neq 0}
is identified as a part of the driving pressure such that there
{\mathcal{P}_{\rm driving}=\Delta V_{\rm eff}}
. Since only the difference
{\mathcal{P}_{\rm driving}-\mathcal{P}_{\rm friction}}
is physical, the identification of driving and frictional pressures is not unique. The identification in Eqs. (
35
) agrees with the convention used in studies of friction on ultrarelativistic bubble walls
[
87
,
88
]
. An advantage of this identification is that the driving force is a constant for a given particle physics model, i.e., independent of the fluid state or microscopic particle processes.

The ultrarelativistic regime.

For
\gamma_{w}\gg 1
, the wall is thin enough in the plasma frame such that one can neglect the collisions between the particles when they cross the wall, this approximation is also called ballistic approximation
[
86
,
57
]
. Mathematically, the ballistic condition is given by

\displaystyle L_{w}\ll\gamma_{w}L_{\rm MFP}\qquad\text{(ballistic condition)}\,,

(43)

where
L_{w}
is the width of the wall in the wall frame,
L_{\rm MFP}
is the mean free path of the incoming particles in the plasma frame. In the wall frame, the plasma moves too fast such that the particles do not have time to collide with each other during the passage of the plasma through the wall. If we do not consider particle-production processes for now, the distribution functions of the plasma remain unchanged even when the latter enters the wall. At this leading-order

\displaystyle f^{(0)}_{i}(\mathbf{k})=f_{i}^{\rm out}(\mathbf{k},T_{+})\,,

(44)

where
f_{i}^{\rm out}
and
T_{+}
are the distribution function and temperature for the plasma in front of the wall, respectively.
11
11

11

In Ref.
[
78
]
, a different notation
f_{i}^{\rm in}(\mathbf{k},T_{+})
is used to denote the distribution of the incoming particles from the outside of the wall. There, the superscript “in” stands for “incoming”.

Furthermore, for ultrarelativistic walls, it is typically assumed that the bubble expansion is in the detonation mode such the particles in front of the wall are in thermal equilibrium at the nucleation temperature,
T_{+}=T_{n}
and
f_{i}^{\rm out}=f_{i}^{\rm out;eq}
.

Therefore, in the ultrarelativistic regime, a first contribution from
\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}
comes from the particles gaining a mass while crossing the wall. Inserting the distribution function of the incoming particles impinging on the wall into Eq. (
36
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}\supset-\sum_{i}\Delta m_{i}^{2}\int_{\mathbf{k},i}\,f_{i}^{\rm out;eq}(\mathbf{k};T_{n})\equiv\mathcal{P}_{\rm BM}\,,

(45)

which is nothing but the Bödeker-Moore thermal friction
[
87
]
, which corresponds to the transition processes we called (1) in the introduction. Notice that in the ballistic regime, the reflection of the particles off the bubble wall can also be accounted for
[
86
,
91
,
83
]
.

It is known that
\mathcal{P}_{\rm BM}
is not the full friction induced by the
\varphi
-dependent mass terms in the ultrarelativistic regime. In fact, due to particle-production processes, e.g. transition radiation, decays and collisions, the distribution functions
f_{i}
are not exactly
f^{(0)}=f_{i}^{\rm out;eq}
. The corrections in principle need to be obtained by solving the EoMs for the two-point functions or, as a pertinent approximation, the Boltzmann equations in the quasi-particle limit. In this paper, we omit this analysis but still reveal the said additional friction contained in
\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}
using some approximations. This will be done in Section
4
.

3.3
Friction induced by condensate-dependent vertices: process (4)

We now turn to the analysis of the contribution to the pressure due to
\varphi
-dependent vertices, as defined in Eq. (
37
). To be specific, let us look at one of the diagrams contributing to the self-energy

\displaystyle\Pi^{ab}_{\varphi}\supset-\frac{\mathrm{i}g^{2}}{2}\Delta_{\phi}^{ab}(x,x^{\prime})(\Delta_{\chi}^{ab}(x,x^{\prime}))^{2}\,.

(46)

Since
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
contains an integral over
z^{\prime}
and thus is non-local, computing it is generally difficult. In Section
5
, we will discuss a localisation procedure for it. In this subsection, we consider a simple situation where the self-energy
\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})
is
z
-translation invariant, i.e.,
{\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})=\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z-z^{\prime})}
. This greatly simplifies the analysis and allows for a clear interpretation of
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
. As we will see shortly,
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
is the friction induced by particle production processes via condensate-particle vertices, e.g. the vertex
g\varphi\phi\chi^{2}
for the diagram in Eq. (
46
).

Translational invariance in the
z
direction is restored as the wall reaches ultrarelativistic velocities, namely as
\gamma_{w}\gg 1
. In this regime, in fact, the following applies:

•

Any particle impinging the bubble wall, say a
\phi
-particle, would have a large
z
-momentum (
\sim\gamma_{w}T
) in the rest frame of the wall, as enforced by the particle’s distribution function. As a result, its
\varphi
-dependent mass, and even its temperature-dependent thermal mass, can be neglected. Consequently, the two-point function
\Delta_{\phi}
approximately exhibits translation invariance in the
z
-direction, i.e.,
\Delta_{\phi}(z,z^{\prime})\approx\Delta_{\phi}(z-z^{\prime})
.

•

In the wall frame, any transition process conserves energy. Therefore, the outgoing states, say two
\chi
-particles, must also have large energy
\sim\gamma_{w}T/2
. Therefore, just like the incoming particles, their
\varphi
-dependent and thermal masses can be neglected, and we have
{\Delta_{\chi}(z,z^{\prime})\approx\Delta_{\chi}(z-z^{\prime})}
.

The above implies that we are also allowed to ignore the coordinate dependence of the distribution functions
f_{\phi/\chi}
, which we will take as functions solely of the three-momentum.
Furthermore, we assume
\chi
-particles to be very heavy

\displaystyle m_{\chi}\gg v_{b}\sim T\,,

(47)

where
v_{b}
is the value of the field
\Phi
in the symmetry-broken phase.
This additional assumption implies that
\chi
-particles are Boltzmann suppressed and thus selects the production process
\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi
over the emission and absorption processes
\phi\chi\to\chi
and
\chi\to\chi\phi
. In the rest frame of the plasma, this process can be described as the bubble wall hitting a
\phi
particle that thus changes its momentum and transits into two
\chi
particles.
The production process has been used as a mechanism of heavy dark matter production, originally proposed in Ref.
[
30
]
, and later generalised to cases of fermion and vector dark matter
[
122
,
39
]
.
Our analysis below, however, can be easily generalised to the case when
\chi
particles are not heavy, as we will comment later in this section (see Eq. (
3.3
) below).
Using the approximate translational invariance along the
z
-direction,

{\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z,z^{\prime})\approx\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z-z^{\prime})}\,,

(48)

we can Fourier transform
\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z-z^{\prime})
with respect to
z-z^{\prime}
. Note that, using Eq. (
31
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{\rm R}(q^{z})=\int\mathrm{d}(z-z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{{-\mathrm{i}q^{z}(z-z^{\prime})}}\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(z-z^{\prime})=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}(x-x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{e}^{{\mathrm{i}\hat{q}(x-x^{\prime})}}\Pi_{\varphi}^{\rm R}(x-x^{\prime})=\widetilde{\Pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(\hat{q})\,,

(49)

where
\hat{q}=(0,0,0,q^{z})
. Note that although
\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}
and
\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}
have different dimensions, their Fourier transforms share the same dimension. Taking the Fourier transform of the wall as well

\displaystyle\varphi(z)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{{\mathrm{i}q^{z}z}}\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})\,,\qquad\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{e}^{-{\mathrm{i}q^{z}z}}\varphi(z)\,,

(50)

with
\widetilde{\varphi}(-q^{z})=\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})^{*}
due to the reality of
\varphi(z)
,
Eq. (
37
) becomes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}\,(\mathrm{i}q^{z})\,|\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})|^{2}\,\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(-q^{z})=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}\,q^{z}\,|\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})|^{2}\,{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(q^{z})\,.}

(51)

Above, we have used the relations
[
123
]

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(q^{z})=-{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(-q^{z})\,,\qquad{\rm Re}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(q^{z})={\rm Re}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(-q^{z})\,,

(52)

and that since we are integrating over a symmetric interval, only the symmetric part of the integrand survives.

It is well known that the imaginary part of self-energies have an interpretation in terms of particle processes via the cutting rules
[
112
,
113
,
114
,
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
]
. To see this, we note that
[
123
]

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\,\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{\rm R}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{>}-\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{<}\right)\,,

(53)

which takes the form of a collision term. Note that the imaginary unit in the above equation is cancelled by the one contained in the self-energies, as can be seen in Eq. (
22
), so that
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
is explicitly real.

Focusing on the contribution to the self-energy in Eq. (
46
), Eq. (
53
) becomes

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(q^{z})\supset

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(\hat{q}-p-k_{1}-k_{2})

\displaystyle\times\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\phi}^{>}(p)\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}_{\chi}(k_{1})\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}_{\chi}(k_{2})-\widetilde{\Delta}_{\phi}^{<}(p)\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}_{\chi}(k_{1})\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}_{\chi}(k_{2})\right]

\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(\hat{q}-p+k_{1}+k_{2})

\displaystyle\times\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\phi}^{>}(p)\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}_{\chi}(k_{1})\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}_{\chi}(k_{2})-\widetilde{\Delta}_{\phi}^{<}(p)\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}_{\chi}(k_{1})\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}_{\chi}(k_{2})\right]\,,

(54)

where we recall that
\hat{q}
has been defined below Eq. (
49
).
In the second step, we have changed the sign of
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
to draw closer to our intuition of an incoming
\phi
particle with momentum
p
decaying into a pair of
\chi
particles with momenta
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
. This is reflected in the relative signs of the momenta inside the
\delta
function; in the final expression of Eq. (
53
) one has the constraint
p-\hat{q}=k_{1}+k_{2}
which has the physical interpretation of a
\phi
particle with momentum
p
which loses or gains q fraction of this momentum,
\hat{q}
, to the wall, before decaying into a pair of
\chi
particles with momenta
k_{1},k_{2}
.
We have also used
{\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}(-k)=\>\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}(k)}
, which follows from taking Fourier transforms of (
11
) with
a,b=+,-
. Equation (
54
) implies the following notation for kinematics,

\displaystyle\phi:\quad\ \ p=(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}},\mathbf{p}_{\perp},p^{z})\,,

(55a)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}:\quad k_{1}=(E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}},\mathbf{k}_{1,\perp},k_{1}^{z})\,,

(55b)

\displaystyle\chi_{2}:\quad k_{2}=(E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}},\mathbf{k}_{2,\perp},k_{2}^{z})\,.

(55c)

We now can use the quasi-particle limit of the Wightman functions in Wigner space given in Eqs. (
25
). Note that the Wigner transform coincides with the Fourier transform when there is full spacetime translation symmetry.
It is convenient to use

\displaystyle\delta(k^{2}-m^{2}_{\phi/\chi})\,\theta(\pm k^{0})=\frac{\delta(k^{0}\mp E_{\mathbf{k}}^{(\phi/\chi)})}{2E_{\mathbf{k}}^{(\phi/\chi)}}\,,

(56)

where
E_{\mathbf{k}}^{(\phi/\chi)}=\sqrt{\mathbf{k}^{2}+m_{\phi/\chi}^{2}}
,
so that we can write the Wightman functions in a more compact form

\displaystyle\Delta^{<}_{\phi/\chi}(p)=\>2\pi\frac{1}{2E^{(\phi/\chi)}_{\mathbf{p}}}\sum_{\alpha=\pm}\delta\left(p^{0}-\alpha E^{(\phi/\chi)}_{\mathbf{p}}\right)\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{2}+f_{\phi/\chi}(\alpha\mathbf{p})\right)\,,

(57a)

\displaystyle\Delta^{>}_{\phi/\chi}(p)=\>2\pi\frac{1}{2E^{(\phi/\chi)}_{\mathbf{p}}}\sum_{\alpha=\pm}\delta\left(p^{0}-\alpha E^{(\phi/\chi)}_{\mathbf{p}}\right)\left(\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+f_{\phi/\chi}(\alpha\mathbf{p})\right)\,.

(57b)

Plugging this into Eq. (
54
), we find

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(q^{z})\supset\>

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}}{4}\sum_{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}=\pm}\int_{\mathbf{p},\phi}\int_{\mathbf{k}_{1},\chi}\int_{\mathbf{k}_{2},\chi}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}-\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2})\,2\pi\,\delta(\alpha E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}-\beta_{1}E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}-\beta_{2}E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}})

\displaystyle\times\Big\{\left[\frac{1+\alpha}{2}+f_{\phi}(\alpha\mathbf{p})\right]\left[\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{2}+f_{\chi}(\beta_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1})\right]\left[\frac{1-\beta_{2}}{2}+f_{\chi}(\beta_{2}\mathbf{k}_{2})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\left[\frac{1-\alpha}{2}+f_{\phi}(\alpha\mathbf{p})\right]\left[\frac{1+\beta_{1}}{2}+f_{\chi}(\beta_{1}\mathbf{k}_{1})\right]\left[\frac{1+\beta_{2}}{2}+f_{\chi}(\beta_{2}\mathbf{k}_{2})\right]\Big\}\,,

(58)

where
\hat{\mathbf{q}}=(0,0,q^{z})
.
The above expression describes all possible processes and the corresponding inverse processes involving one
\phi
particle and two
\chi
particles, corresponding to the second diagram in Eq. (
21
).
In the following, we will avoid specifying the particle species in the integrals, since it is clear that momentum
\mathbf{p}
refers to the
\phi
particle and momenta
\mathbf{k}_{1}
and
\mathbf{k}_{2}
to the
\chi
particles.

To simplify our expression, we notice that the combinations
{\{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}=\{+,-,-\}}
and
{\{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}=\{-,+,+\}}
are excluded, since for these the energy conserving
\delta
-function vanishes.
Let us write the remaining combinations, ignoring the integral and the three-momentum conserving
\delta
-function for the time being

\displaystyle\delta\left(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}\right)\Big\{\underbrace{[1+f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})]f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{1})f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{2})}_{\chi-\text{pair annihilation}}-\underbrace{f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})[1+f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{1})][1+f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{2})]}_{\chi-\text{pair production}}\Big\}

\displaystyle+\delta\left(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}+E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}\right)\Big\{\underbrace{(1+f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p}))(1+f_{\chi}(-\mathbf{k}_{1}))f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{2})}_{\phi-\text{emission}}-\underbrace{f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})f_{\chi}(-\mathbf{k}_{1})(1+f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{2}))}_{\phi-\text{absorption}}\Big\}

\displaystyle+\delta\left(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}+E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}\right)\Big\{\underbrace{(1+f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p}))(1+f_{\chi}(-\mathbf{k}_{2}))f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{1})}_{\phi-\text{emission}}-\underbrace{f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})f_{\chi}(-\mathbf{k}_{2})(1+f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k}_{1}))}_{\phi-\text{absorption}}\Big\}

\displaystyle-\left(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2}\longleftrightarrow-\mathbf{p},-\mathbf{k}_{1},-\mathbf{k}_{2}\right)\,.

(59)

Note that the expression is anti-symmetric under
{\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2}\}\to\{-\mathbf{p},-\mathbf{k}_{1},-\mathbf{k}_{2}\}}
, making the entire contribution anti-symmetric under
{\hat{\mathbf{q}}\to-\hat{\mathbf{q}}}
, as expected. In Eq. (
3.3
) we have labelled the different contributions with their physical interpretation, which can be read off from the
\delta
function constraint on the energies, as well as the fact that initial states appear weighted with the number distribution functions
f_{X}
, while final states are weighted with Bose-enhancement factors
1+f_{X}
.
Note that here the Bose-enhancement factors are not inserted ad hoc, but appear automatically in the formalism. Furthermore, it is easy to check that the above physical interpretation matches with the 3 momentum constraint
\delta^{(3)}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}-\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2})
appearing in (
3.3
). For example consider the contribution labelled as “
\chi
-pair production”, which is proportional to
f_{\phi}({\bf p})(1+f_{\chi}({\bf k}_{1}))(1+f_{\chi}({\bf k}_{2}))
. Reading off the three-momenta appearing in the distribution functions, this has the natural interpretation of an initial
\phi
particle with three-momentum
{\bf p}
emitting two
\chi
particles with momenta
\bf k_{1}
and
\bf k_{2}
. This is compatible with the
\delta^{(3)}
constraint enforcing
{\bf p}-\hat{\mathbf{q}}=\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}
, where
-\hat{\mathbf{q}}
can be interpreted as momentum taken away from the wall. The rest of the contributions in Eq. (
3.3
) can also be interpreted in a similar way.

Observe that if the
\chi
-particles are very heavy, namely
m_{\chi}^{2}\gg m_{\phi}^{2}
, the particle density
f_{\chi}(\mathbf{k})
is strongly suppressed for momenta of the scale of the phase transition temperature
\beta^{-1}\sim m_{\phi}
.
Then, all terms containing
f_{\chi}
are exponentially suppressed, and the only process that plays a role is the decay of
\phi
particles, namely the
\chi
pair production.

Then, under the assumption that
m^{2}_{\chi}\gg m_{\phi}^{2}\sim T^{2}
, we can drop all terms except the one describing the pair production of
\chi
particles, and our expression simplifies enormously

\displaystyle\left[{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}(q^{z})\right]_{\phi\to\chi\chi}\approx

\displaystyle\frac{g^{2}}{4}\int_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2}}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(3)}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}-\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2})\,\delta(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}})\Big[f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})-f_{\phi}(-\mathbf{p})\Big]\,.

(60)

Substituting Eqs. (
60
) into Eq. (
51
), and using the
\delta
function over the momentum along the
z
direction to localise the
q
integral, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}\supset\mathcal{P}_{\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi}\equiv\>

\displaystyle\frac{g^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(2)}(\mathbf{p}_{\perp}-\mathbf{k}_{1,\perp}-\mathbf{k}_{2,\perp})\delta(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}})\,f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})\,\Delta p^{z}\,|\widetilde{\varphi}(\Delta p^{z})|^{2}\,,

(61)

where

\displaystyle\Delta p^{z}=-(p^{z}-k_{1}^{z}-k_{2}^{z})\,.

(62)

Equation (
61
) agrees precisely with the expression derived in Refs.
[
91
,
94
]
. Note that above we have defined
\Delta p^{z}
with an additional factor of
-1
.
The sign is different because we define the wall to expand in the positive
z
direction, while in Refs.
[
91
,
94
]
, it is defined to expand in the negative
z
direction. The quantity
\Delta p^{z}
in (
62
) corresponds to
-q^{z}
inside
{\rm Im}\tilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi}
(see Eq. (
60
)) which in the case of the
\chi
pair production we identified with the momentum taken away from the wall. A momentum loss, as required to compensate the driving force and reach a stationary state, corresponds to
-q^{z}=\Delta p^{z}>0
giving a positive
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
, fitting our conventions in which the balance of forces is written as
\Delta V=\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}+\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}>0
.
In the expression (
61
) for the force, the quantity
g^{2}|\tilde{\varphi}(\Delta p^{z})|^{2}
can be interpreted as squared transition amplitude
|\mathcal{M}_{\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi}|^{2}
for an incoming
\phi
particle to extract momentum
\Delta p^{z}
from the wall and split into two particles. To give the total force, this probability has to be weighted with the density of incoming particles, multiplied by the momentum lost by the wall and integrated over the phase space of the final
\chi
particles, subject to the appropriate energy and momentum conservation constraints.
In Appendix
A
we further simplify the expression for the pressure due to pair production by using the remaining
\delta
functions. We arrive at the following general formula

\mathcal{P}_{\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi}=\>\frac{g^{2}}{32\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbf{p}}f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})\times\left[\vartheta(-p^{z}-\Delta)-\vartheta(p^{z}-\Delta)\right]\int_{q_{-}}^{q_{+}}\mathrm{d}q^{z}\,q^{z}|\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})|^{2}\sqrt{1-\frac{4m_{\chi}^{2}}{m_{\phi}^{2}+2q^{z}|p^{z}|-(q^{z})^{2}}}\,,

(63)

where the bounds of the
q
integral are
q_{\pm}=\>|p^{z}|\pm\sqrt{(p^{z})^{2}-\Delta^{2}}
, and we have defined
{\Delta^{2}=4m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{\phi}^{2}}
.
Equation (
63
) lends itself to numerical evaluation.
In Fig.
3
we show the contribution to the pressure due to the pair production of heavy particles as a function of the relativistic boost factor
\gamma_{w}
, for different values of the mass of the incoming particle
m_{\phi}
. We have taken here a tanh wall profile, as defined in Eq. (
A181
). The temperature
T
has been chosen as the reference scale, and we show the result for reference values of
L_{w}
and
m_{\chi}
.
First, we observe that for all values of
m_{\phi}
, the pressure asymptotically grows logarithmically with
\gamma_{w}
, and different slopes imply different pre-factors of the logarithm.
The logarithmic growth was observed numerically in Ref.
[
94
]
for
m_{\phi}=0
, and we demonstrate it analytically at the end of this section, by studying the ultrarelativistic limit.
As the value of
m_{\phi}
grows, the pressure decreases because of the suppression due to the statistical distribution.

Figure 3
:
Pressure induced by pair production obtained via the numerical evaluation of Eq. (
63
) varying the mass of the incoming particle
\phi
. Reference values
T=1
,
L_{w}=1
and
m_{\chi}=5
.

Accordingly, the self-energy in Eq. (
22
) and therefore the friction from Eq. (
37
) also receives contributions from processes involving three
\phi
-particles.
An analogous derivation to the one just done leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}\supset\mathcal{P}_{\phi\phi\phi}\equiv\>

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\phi}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(2)}(\mathbf{p}_{\perp}-\mathbf{k}_{1,\perp}-\mathbf{k}_{2,\perp})\delta(E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{p}}-E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{1}}-E^{(\phi)}_{\mathbf{k}_{2}})\,\Delta p^{z}\,|\widetilde{\varphi}(\Delta p^{z})|^{2}

\displaystyle\times\Big[f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p})(1+f_{\phi}(\mathbf{k}_{1}))(1+f_{\phi}(\mathbf{k}_{2}))-(1+f_{\phi}(\mathbf{p}))f_{\phi}(\mathbf{k}_{1})f_{\phi}(\mathbf{k}_{2})\Big]\,,

(64)

where no term has been dropped, and no approximation has been made. The above expression for the force captures the momentum lost by the wall from the splittings of incoming
\phi
particles or the annihilation of incoming pairs of particles.
In Eq. (
3.3
), the three-momentum integrals all refer to the particle species
\phi
.

The ultrarelativistic regime.

As
\gamma_{w}
grows larger, we can obtain an analytical asymptotic expression for Eq. (
63
). The computation is presented step by step in Appendix
A
, leading to the final result

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{w}\to\infty}_{\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{g^{2}v_{b}^{2}T^{2}}{24\times 32\pi^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\gamma_{w}T}{2\pi L_{w}m_{\chi}^{2}}\right)\vartheta\bigg(\frac{\gamma_{w}T}{2\pi L_{w}m_{\chi}^{2}}-1\bigg)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{g^{2}v_{b}^{2}\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{w}\rightarrow\infty}_{\phi\to\phi}}{32\pi^{2}\Delta m_{\phi}^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\gamma_{w}T}{2\pi L_{w}m_{\chi}^{2}}\right)\vartheta\bigg(\frac{\gamma_{w}T}{2\pi L_{w}m_{\chi}^{2}}-1\bigg)\,,

(65)

where in the last line, we rephrased the pressure from pair production in terms of the pressure from the mass gain of
\phi
, namely
{\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{w}\rightarrow\infty}_{\phi\to\phi}=\Delta m_{\phi}^{2}T^{2}/24}
.
The
\vartheta
-function has been introduced to make it explicit that this expression is only asymptotically valid and breaks down when
{\pi Lm_{\chi}^{2}/(\gamma_{w}T)\to 1}
.
Equation (
65
) demonstrates the logarithmic growth with
\gamma_{w}T
observed already in Fig.
3
.

Phenomenology of friction from pair production

During a dark sector FOPT, the pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi}
can dominate over
\mathcal{P}_{\phi\to\phi}
for
{g^{2}v_{b}^{2}\gtrsim 32\pi^{2}\Delta m^{2}_{\phi}}
. This condition can be satisfied if the change of the mass of
\phi
particles is loop-suppressed. For example, the quartic coupling of the
\Phi
field could be dominated by the one-loop contribution of the heavy
\chi
field,
(1/4!)\lambda_{\phi,\rm 1-loop}\sim(1/4)g^{2}/16\pi^{2}\gg\lambda_{\phi}/4!
.
This would lead to
\Delta m_{\phi}^{2}\sim 3g^{2}v_{b}^{2}/16\pi^{2}
.
In this case,
\mathcal{P}_{\phi\rightarrow\chi\chi}
dominates over
\mathcal{P}_{\phi\rightarrow\phi}
for large
\gamma_{w}
.
This simple case is a natural realisation where the pressure from heavy particles becomes relevant.

During a first-order electroweak phase transition, one identifies the
\phi
particle with the Higgs
h
. There are two unavoidable particle production processes: 1) the pair production of Higgs from one incoming Higgs particle,
h\to hh
,
hh\to h
and 2) the pair production of gauge bosons from an incoming Higgs
h\to W^{+}W^{-},ZZ
and their permutations, e.g.,
Z\rightarrow hZ
. The main difference from the computation above is that both the Higgs and the gauge bosons are light particles. Consequently, all these particles are not Boltzmann suppressed in the plasma. A more precise evaluation would require numerical methods, starting with the expression in Eq. (
3.3
).

4
Mixing and transition radiation frictions from the VIA

In the previous sections, we have studied the friction induced by the elementary type (1)
1\rightarrow 1
process and type (4)
1\rightarrow 2
particle production. In this section, we study the contribution from the type (2)
1\rightarrow 1
mixing and type (3)
1\rightarrow 2
transition radiation. As we mentioned earlier, these contributions are in principle captured by
\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}
, once we insert the full propagators
\Delta
found as solutions of the EoMs for the two-point functions or, as a suitable approximation, of the Boltzmann equations derived in the quasi-particle limit and at the leading order of the gradient expansion.
In practice, this is only feasible numerically.
To reveal how mixing and transition radiation are captured by
\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}
in the formalism we employ, we perform an expansion of the two-point functions in the background field
\varphi
, and show how these contributions are encoded inside higher-loop self-energy terms.
This expansion amounts to treating the
\varphi
-dependent mass terms as perturbation vertices, an approach known as VEV insertion approximation or VIA. Of course, keeping only a few terms in the expansion does not give us the full result that one would obtain by solving the Boltzmann equations, but it is sufficient for our purposes.

4.1
Pressure from scalar mixing: process (2)

The model considered so far assumes a diagonal mass matrix. In the presence of mass mixing, it was shown in
[
91
]
that the transition from a light state to a heavy state can also induce friction. In this section, we show how this friction from the
1\rightarrow 1
mixing process is captured from our general framework. In another context, mass mixing was shown to be a new source of baryogenesis for electroweak baryogenesis at slow walls
[
124
,
125
]
and fast walls
[
14
]
.

As an illustrative example, we consider the theory of two real scalar fields interacting via the bubble background
\varphi
. The potential is then written as

\displaystyle-\mathcal{L}_{\rm mix}=\kappa\,\varphi\chi s+\frac{1}{2}m_{s}^{2}s^{2}+\frac{1}{2}m_{\chi}^{2}\chi^{2}\,,

(66)

where
\kappa
is a coupling of mass dimension one, and the kinetic terms are implied. The extension of the 2PI as written in Eq. (
3.1
) is straightforward. We assume that
\chi
is heavy (
m_{\chi}\gg T
) so that
\chi
particles are Boltzmann suppressed outside of the bubble. The
s
particles are light (
m_{s}<T
) so they are abundant in the plasma.
During the expansion of the bubble, a light
s
particle passing through the wall can transit to a heavy
\chi
particle due to the mixing term
\kappa\varphi\chi s
, inducing a contribution to the friction on the wall. Below, we derive this contribution.

4.1.1
Light-heavy case.

We shall show that the friction from mixing can be understood from the following term in the effective action,

\displaystyle D[\varphi]\equiv\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}{\rm Tr}\left[G^{-1}(\varphi)\Delta\right]=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{ij}\sum_{a,\,b}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\left.\left[G_{ij}^{ab,-1}(\varphi(x))\Delta_{ji}^{ba}(x,y)\right]\right|_{y\,=\,x}

(67)

where
i,j=\chi,s
are flavour indices, and we recall that
a,b=\pm
are Keldysh polarisation indices.
In the flavour basis,
\Delta^{ab}
with fixed
a,b
is a matrix of the form

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm flavor}^{ab}=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta^{ab}_{\chi\chi}&\Delta^{ab}_{\chi s}\\
\Delta^{ab}_{s\chi}&\Delta^{ab}_{ss}\end{pmatrix}

(68)

and the quadratic fluctuation operator reads

\displaystyle G_{\rm flavor}^{ab,-1}=\mathrm{i}c^{ab}\begin{pmatrix}\Box+m_{\chi}^{2}&\kappa\varphi^{a}\\
\kappa\varphi^{a}&\Box+m_{s}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(69)

with
c=\mathrm{diag}(+1,-1)
.

We are interested in the terms explicitly depending on
\varphi
which contribute to the condensate EoM. These are

\displaystyle D[\varphi]\supset

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\kappa\varphi^{+}(x)\left(\Delta^{++}_{\chi s}(x,x)+\Delta^{++}_{s\chi}(x,x)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\kappa\varphi^{-}(x)\left(\Delta_{\chi s}^{--}(x,x)+\Delta_{s\chi}^{--}(x,x)\right)\,,

(70)

In the condensate EoM (
20
), the above contributes to a term

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\delta D[\varphi]}{\delta\varphi^{+}(x)}\right|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}\supset\>\frac{1}{2}\kappa\left.\left(\Delta^{++}_{\chi s}(x,x;\varphi)+\Delta^{++}_{s\chi}(x,x;\varphi)\right)\right|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}\,.

(71)

Note that we have indicated here an implicit dependence of the two-point functions
\Delta
on the background field, i.e. the one-point function,
\varphi
. The effective action is a functional of the two-point and one-point functions, which are independent. However, when extremizing the effective action, we obtain the EoMs that are coupled integro-differential equations for the one-point and the two-point functions. Here we use an approximation where the dependence of the one-point function on the two-point functions is neglected—above, on the RHS we have assumed that the two-point functions have been obtained from their EoMs and thus obtain an implicit dependence on the one-point functions
\varphi^{a}
.

Now one can expand
\Delta^{++}_{\chi s}(x_{1},x_{2};\varphi)
and
\Delta^{++}_{s\chi}(x_{1},x_{2};\varphi)
in
\varphi^{a}
, and we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{++}_{\chi s}(x_{1},x_{2};\varphi)=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{a}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,a\,\kappa\,\varphi^{a}(x^{\prime})\Delta^{+a}_{\chi\chi}(x_{1},x^{\prime};0)\Delta^{a+}_{ss}(x^{\prime},x_{2};0)+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{\kappa\varphi}{m_{\chi}^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\,,

(72)

where
\Delta_{\chi\chi}(x_{1},x_{2};0)
and
\Delta_{ss}(x_{1},x_{2};0)
are the two-point functions obtained by solving the Schwinger–Dyson equations with
\varphi=0
. We confirm explicitly in Appendix
B
that the expansion in Eq. (
72
) is the solution at leading order of the exact kinetic equation for the two-point functions. A similar expansion applies for
\Delta^{++}_{s\chi}(x_{1},x_{2};\varphi)
.

After this expansion, we obtain a self-energy-like term,

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\delta D[\varphi]}{\delta\varphi^{+}(x)}\right|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}\supset

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\,\kappa\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\kappa\varphi(x^{\prime})\left[\Delta_{\chi\chi}^{++}(x,x^{\prime};0)\Delta_{ss}^{++}(x^{\prime},x;0)-\Delta_{\chi\chi}^{+-}(x,x^{\prime};0)\Delta_{ss}^{-+}(x^{\prime},x;0)\right]

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\rm mix}(x,x^{\prime})\varphi(x^{\prime})\,.

(73)

where in the last line, we have defined the
induced
self-energy

\displaystyle\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\rm mix}(x,x^{\prime})=-\mathrm{i}\kappa^{2}\left[\Delta^{++}_{\chi\chi}(x,x^{\prime};0)\Delta^{++}_{ss}(x,x^{\prime};0)-\Delta_{\chi\chi}^{+-}(x,x^{\prime};0)\Delta_{ss}^{+-}(x,x^{\prime};0)\right]\,.

(74)

The above induced self-energy can be seen to arise from contributions to the effective action of the following form,

(75)

where a dashed/solid line represents
\Delta_{\chi\chi}(0)/\Delta_{ss}(0)
. The dots represent the “vertex” given by the mixing term
\kappa\varphi\chi s
and can be of either
+
or
-
type. Note that this diagram is two-particle-
reducible
. However, it does not come from
\Gamma_{2}
in the 2PI effective action but only emerges from the one-loop term
D[\varphi]
after the field expansion.

Similar to
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
in Eq. (
37
), the new self-energy term in Eq. (
4.1.1
) induces a pressure

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mix}=-\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi}{\mathrm{d}z}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\rm mix}(z,z^{\prime})\varphi(z^{\prime})\,,

(76)

where
\pi_{\varphi;\rm mix}^{\rm R}
is obtained from Eq. (
31
) with
\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi}
replaced by
\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\rm mix}
.

Ignoring the plasma inhomogeneities in the
z
-direction or assuming large
\gamma_{w}
,
\Delta_{\chi\chi}(x,x^{\prime};0)
and
\Delta_{ss}(x,x^{\prime};0)
have spacetime-translation invariance, noting that we do not have additional
\varphi
-dependent mass terms since we are working within the VIA. Then, in analogy with the discussion in Section
3.3
and Eq.(
51
), Eq. (
76
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mix}=-\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}\,q^{z}\,|\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})|^{2}\,{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\rm mix}(q^{z})\,.

(77)

Following the derivation of Eq. (
61
), we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mix}=

\displaystyle-\kappa^{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}q^{z}|\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})|^{2}\int_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})\,2\pi\delta(E^{(s)}_{\mathbf{p}}-E^{(\chi)}_{\mathbf{k}})\left[f_{s}(\mathbf{p})-f_{s}(-\mathbf{p})\right]

\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle-\kappa^{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}q^{z}|\widetilde{\varphi}(q^{z})|^{2}\int_{\mathbf{p}}\frac{1}{2E_{\mathbf{p}}^{(s)}}2\pi\delta(E_{\mathbf{p}}^{(s)}-E_{\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}}^{(\chi)})\left[f_{s}(\mathbf{p})-f_{s}(-\mathbf{p})\right]\,,

(78)

where we have assumed that the
\chi
-particles are very heavy and thus absent in the plasma.
We can derive an analytical estimate of the pressure due to mixing in the ultrarelativistic limit following the same steps as explained in Appendix
A
for the pressure due to pair production.
First, we use the energy conserving
\delta
function to fully localise the
q^{z}
integral, and in doing that imposes a constraint on
p^{z}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm mix}=\>

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha=\pm}\int\mathrm{d}p^{z}\vartheta((p^{z})^{2}-m_{\chi}^{2}+m_{s}^{2})\frac{q_{\alpha}^{z}}{q^{z}_{\alpha}-p^{z}}|\widetilde{\varphi}(q_{\alpha}^{z})|^{2}F(p^{z})

\displaystyle=\>

\displaystyle-\kappa^{2}\int_{\Delta}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}p^{z}G_{\rm mix}(p^{z})F(p^{z})\,.

(79)

In the second step, we have used symmetry of the argument under
p^{z}\to-p^{z}
to restrict the integration domain. We have defined
\Delta^{2}=m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2}
and the momentum transfer

q^{z}_{\pm}=\>p^{z}\pm\sqrt{(p^{z})^{2}-m_{\chi}^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}\,.

(80)

Function
F
follows the definition of Eq. (
A164a
), and function
G_{\rm mix}
is defined as follows

G_{\rm mix}(p^{z})=\>\sum_{\alpha=\pm}\frac{q_{\alpha}^{z}}{q^{z}_{\alpha}-p^{z}}|\widetilde{\varphi}(q_{\alpha}^{z})|^{2}\,.

(81)

Equation (
79
) is the full pressure due to mixing, valid also away from the ultrarelativistic limit.
Using equilibrium distributions in the wall frame, the function
F
can be computed analytically as shown in Eq. (
A173
), upon making the exchange
{m_{\phi}\to m_{s}}
.
In the ultrarelativistic limit, the mixing pressure can be evaluated in terms of a cumulant expansion, as explained in Appendix
A
for the pressure due to pair production in Eq. (
A170
).
Following the same procedure for the pressure due to mixing we find

\mathcal{P}_{\rm mix}^{\gamma_{w}\to\infty}=\>-\kappa^{2}\mathcal{N}_{F}G_{\rm mix}(\langle p^{z}\rangle)\,.

(82)

The normalisation
\mathcal{N}_{F}
and the average
z
momentum
\langle p^{z}\rangle
are computed in Appendix
A

\mathcal{N}_{F}=\>\frac{T^{2}}{24}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{w}^{-1})\right)\,,\qquad\langle p^{z}\rangle=\>\sigma\gamma_{w}T\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{w}^{-1})\right)\,,

(83)

where the proportionality constant is
\sigma=12\zeta_{3}/\pi^{2}
.
Since the wall Fourier transform
\widetilde{\varphi}
is strongly suppressed for large arguments, when computing
{G_{\rm mix}(\sigma\gamma_{w}T)}
only the one with
\alpha=-
in Eq. (
81
) survives.
Using a tanh wall and expanding at leading order in
\gamma_{w}
we find

\mathcal{L}_{w}m_{\chi}^{2}/T\lesssim\gamma_{w}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\rm mix}^{\gamma_{w}\to\infty}=\>\frac{2\kappa^{2}v_{b}^{2}}{m_{\chi}^{2}}\frac{T^{2}}{24}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{w}^{-1})\right)\,,

(84)

where we have also taken the limit
m_{\chi}\gg m_{s}
.
Note that this result applies to general wall profiles. In fact, the only properties of the Fourier transformed wall profile
\widetilde{\varphi}(q)
we have used are that it is exponentially suppressed for large
q
and that it falls as
1/q
near the origin. The first is satisfied if the field approaches the minima
\varphi=0
and
\varphi=v_{b}
exponentially fast away from the wall. This is always valid for problems with a characteristic scale, here
T
.
The second property is equivalent to asking that for large length scales the field profile looks like a step function, which is generally true for a background field interpolating between the two minima.

Equation (
84
) matches the result obtained in Eq. (81) of Ref.
[
91
]
up to a factor of two, where our
\kappa
equals to
2B
of Ref.
[
91
]
. The same analysis can be applied to the case of fermion mixing, presented in Appendix
C
. In Fig.
4
we show the mixing pressure computed via numerical evaluation of Eq. (
79
) compared to the ultrarelativistic asymptotic expression of Eq. (
84
).
We find good agreement between the two when
\gamma_{w}
is very large. As the wall becomes less relativistic, the pressure is suppressed and the asymptotic approximation breaks down for values
L_{w}m_{\chi}^{2}/T\gtrsim\gamma_{w}
.

Figure 4
:
Pressure due to mixing as defined in Eq. (
79
), compared to the asymptotic result valid for ultrarelativistic walls obtained in Eq. (
84
). The reference values are
T=1
,
L_{w}=1
and
m_{\chi}=5
.

If there is a
\varphi
-dependent mass term for
s
, which gives a gain in its squared mass
\Delta m^{2}_{s}
upon entering the bubble, then there is also the Bödeker-Moore pressure
[
87
]
(cf. Eq. (
45
)) for the elementary
1\rightarrow 1
process of
s
-particles,
\mathcal{P}_{\rm BM,s}\approx\Delta m_{s}^{2}T^{2}/24
. However, the contribution to the pressure from the mixing can dominant over
\mathcal{P}_{\rm BM,s}
if
\Delta m^{2}_{s}/v^{2}_{b}\ll\kappa^{2}/m^{2}_{\chi}
.

4.1.2
Light-light case.

We now turn to the case when both scalar fields have a small mass
{m_{s}\;\raisebox{-3.00003pt}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\displaystyle<}}{{\sim}}$}\;m_{\chi}\lesssim T}
, which can thus be neglected in the computation. In this case, we directly work in the mass eigenspace of states.
The first step is to diagonalise the inverse propagator in Eq. (
69
) and then go to the mass eigenbasis with a unitary transformation
U
:
\chi,s\to\chi^{\prime},s^{\prime}
. Doing so, we obtain the two eigenstates

\displaystyle M^{2}_{1,2}=\frac{m_{s}^{2}+m_{\chi}^{2}}{2}\pm\frac{(m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})}{2}\sqrt{1+4(\kappa\varphi)^{2}/(m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})^{2}}

(85)

and the inverse propagator can be written in the form

\displaystyle G^{-1}_{\rm mass}=\mathrm{i}c^{ab}\begin{pmatrix}\Box+M_{1}^{2}&0\\
0&\Box+M_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(86)

The one-loop term in the EoM for the condensate can then be written in the mass eigenbasis as

\displaystyle D[\varphi]\equiv\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}{\rm Tr}\left[G_{{\rm mass}}^{-1}(\varphi)\Delta_{\rm mass}\right]=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{a,\,b}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\left.\left[G^{ab,-1}_{i}(\varphi(x))\Delta_{i}^{ba}(x,y)\right]\right|_{y\,=\,x}\,,

(87)

where now
i=1,2
corresponds to the two mass eigenstates. The
\varphi
-dependent part is

\displaystyle D[\varphi]\supset

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})}{2}\sqrt{1+4(\kappa\varphi^{+})^{2}/(m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})^{2}}(\Delta^{++}_{1}-\Delta^{++}_{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\frac{(m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})}{2}\sqrt{1+4(\kappa\varphi^{-})^{2}/(m_{\chi}^{2}-m_{s}^{2})^{2}}(\Delta^{--}_{1}-\Delta^{--}_{2})\,.

(88)

The two terms in the sum contribute to the EoM of the condensate
\varphi^{\pm}
, reflecting that we can consistently have
\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}
. If the two particles have
m_{\chi}\approx m_{s}\lesssim T
, then they have roughly identical thermal distributions, leading to
{\Delta^{++}_{1}-\Delta^{++}_{2}\to 0}
, and consequently the pressure from such mixing is suppressed. Therefore, we see that to have a sizeable mixing pressure, one of the states should be Boltzmann suppressed.
As a cross-check, it can be seen that when carrying out the computation with the VIA approach but dropping the assumption of a heavy
\chi
particle, the resulting pressure goes to zero in the limit
m_{\chi}\rightarrow m_{s}
, because the kinematics enforces a zero value of the momentum loss of the wall,
q^{z}
.

4.2
Pressure from transition radiation: process (3)

To extract the contribution to the pressure due to the transition radiation, we now study the dynamics of a fermion
\psi
and a gauge boson
A_{\mu}
, the latter being coupled to the condensate. The relevant part in the Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\supset-g_{1}\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}A^{\mu}\psi+\frac{g_{2}^{2}\Phi^{2}}{4}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}\,.

(89)

We take the
\psi
-particles to be light (
m_{\psi}\sim T
) so that they are abundant in the plasma during the transition, and also assume that they couple weakly to the condensate so that they do not change mass appreciably during the phase transition. A particle having these properties is, for example, the electron during the electroweak phase transition.

4.2.1
Transition radiation and its relation to the CTP formalism

When the fermion passes through the wall, it can emit a gauge boson due to the
\varphi
-dependent mass of the latter,
12
12

12

Transition radiation can happen if either the fermion or the gauge boson has a
\varphi
-dependent mass. We focus on this second scenario, as it is known that only this case brings a
\gamma_{w}
increasing pressure
[
88
,
92
]
.
giving
\psi\rightarrow\psi A
, in a way analogous to a charged particle crossing a boundary separating two media with different refractive index and thus emitting light.
As the bubble expands, particles enter it and gain momentum, which then results in transition radiation, thus inducing additional friction on the wall.

Perhaps unexpectedly, the contribution of the transition radiation to the friction becomes particularly relevant when the wall becomes highly relativistic and was found, within the ballistic approximation, to scale like
\gamma_{w}

[
88
]
. From a phenomenological point of view, this contribution is crucial as it almost excludes the possibility of runaway solutions for gauged phase transitions. This has very important consequences on the shape of the gravitational wave spectrum, the yield of baryogenesis and the production of dark matter during bubble expansion.

Similarly to the mixing pressure, the pressure from the transition radiation process is, in principle, captured by
\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}
.
However, to see this without solving the Boltzmann equations, we again perform an expansion of the two-point functions of the gauge boson in insertions of the background
\varphi
. The diagram responsible for the emission of the gauge bosons is

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu\nu,++}_{A}(x,x;\varphi)\supset\hbox to72.07pt{\vbox to39.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.94658pt\lower-16.89224pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(90)

This generates new diagrams in the effective action that are not necessarily two-particle-irreducible. We will see that the friction caused by the transition radiation can be understood analogously to
\mathcal{P}_{\rm vertex}
from the induced self-energy terms after the field expansion.

Although VIA is useful for revealing transition radiation without solving the Boltzmann equations, it has some limitations. When treating
g_{2}^{2}\varphi^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}
as a perturbation vertex term, the gauge boson has a
\varphi
-independent dispersion relation. And this means that for the elementary
1\to 1
process in the VIA, the gauge boson can either enter the bubble without momentum exchange with the wall, or be reflected. This is, of course, an artefact of the VIA. The mass-gain process cannot be captured by any finite-order truncation in the VIA, and must be computed with the original term
\mathcal{P}_{\rm mass}
.

4.2.2
Computation of the transition radiation in the CTP formalism

We now present the complete derivation of the transition radiation in the CTP+2PI formalism. We start by writing down the two-point functions of the fermions and gauge bosons. The fermion two-point functions on the CTP contour are defined as

\displaystyle S^{<}_{\psi}(x,y)\equiv S_{\psi}^{+-}(x,y)=\langle T_{\mathcal{C}}\psi^{+}(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{-}(y)\rangle=-\langle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(y)\psi(x)\rangle\,,

(91a)

\displaystyle S^{>}_{\psi}(x,y)\equiv S_{\psi}^{-+}(x,y)=\langle T_{\mathcal{C}}\psi^{-}(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{+}(y)\rangle=\langle\psi(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(y)\rangle\,,

(91b)

\displaystyle S^{T}_{\psi}(x,y)\equiv S_{\psi}^{++}(x,y)=\langle T_{\mathcal{C}}\psi^{+}(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{+}(y)\rangle=\langle T\psi(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(y)\rangle\,,

(91c)

\displaystyle S^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\psi}(x,y)\equiv S_{\psi}^{--}(x,y)=\langle T_{\mathcal{C}}\psi^{-}(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{-}(y)\rangle=\langle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\psi(x)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\psi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(y)\rangle\,.

(91d)

In the on-shell limit and at the leading order in the gradient expansion, these read in Wigner space
\{k,x\}

[
120
]
13
13

13

Again, note the difference in the notation; Our
S_{\psi}
is the
\mathrm{i}S_{\psi}
in Ref.
[
120
]
.

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\psi}^{<}(k,x)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m_{\psi}^{2})(\not{k}+m_{\psi})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f_{\psi}(\mathbf{k},x)-\vartheta(-k^{0})(1-\bar{f}_{\psi}(-\mathbf{k},x))\right]\,,

(92a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\psi}^{>}(k,x)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m_{\psi}^{2})(\not{k}+m_{\psi})\left[-\vartheta(k^{0})(1-f_{\psi}(\mathbf{k},x))+\vartheta(-k^{0})\bar{f}_{\psi}(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(92b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\psi}^{T}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm i}(\not{k}+m_{\psi})}{k^{2}-m_{\psi}^{2}+{\rm i}\varepsilon}-2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m_{\psi}^{2})(\not{k}+m_{\psi})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f_{\psi}(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})\bar{f}_{\psi}(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(92c)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\psi}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{-{\rm i}(\not{k}+m_{\psi})}{k^{2}-m_{\psi}^{2}-{\rm i}\varepsilon}-2\pi\delta(k^{2}-m_{\psi}^{2})(\not{k}+m_{\psi})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f_{\psi}(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})\bar{f}_{\psi}(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(92d)

where
f_{\psi}
is the distribution function for fermions and
\bar{f}_{\psi}
for anti-fermions.
We can define analogously the gauge-boson two-point functions on the CTP contour
{\langle T_{\mathcal{C}}A^{\mu}A^{\nu}\rangle}
, which in Wigner space read

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{A}^{\mu\nu,<}(k,x)

\displaystyle=2\pi P^{\mu\nu}\delta(k^{2}-m_{A}^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f_{A}(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})(1+f_{A}(-\mathbf{k},x))\right]\,,

(93a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{A}^{\mu\nu,>}(k,x)

\displaystyle=2\pi P^{\mu\nu}\delta(k^{2}-m_{A}^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})(1+f_{A}(\mathbf{k},x))+\vartheta(-k^{0})f_{A}(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(93b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{A}^{\mu\nu,T}(k,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}P^{\mu\nu}}{k^{2}-m_{A}^{2}+{\rm i}\varepsilon}+2\pi P^{\mu\nu}\delta(k^{2}-m_{A}^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f_{A}(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})f_{A}(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(93c)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Delta\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{A}^{\mu\nu,\bar{T}}(k,x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}P^{\mu\nu}}{k^{2}-m_{A}^{2}-{\rm i}\varepsilon}+2\pi P^{\mu\nu}\delta(k^{2}-m_{A}^{2})\left[\vartheta(k^{0})f_{A}(\mathbf{k},x)+\vartheta(-k^{0})f_{A}(-\mathbf{k},x)\right]\,,

(93d)

where

\displaystyle P^{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\lambda}\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(k)\epsilon^{\nu,*}_{\lambda}(k)\,,

(94)

and
\epsilon_{\lambda}
are the two transversal polarisation vectors, labelled by
\lambda
, of the gauge bosons emitted on shell.

Again, we start by looking at the one-loop term in the 2PI effective action,

\displaystyle D_{A}[\varphi]\equiv\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}{\rm Tr}\left[G_{A}^{-1}(\varphi)\Delta_{A}\right]=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{\mu,\nu}\sum_{a,\,b}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\left.\left[G_{A,\mu\nu}^{ab,-1}(\varphi(x))\Delta_{A}^{\nu\mu,ba}(x,y)\right]\right|_{y\,=\,x}\,,

(95)

where
G_{A,\mu\nu}^{ab,-1}(\varphi)=-\mathrm{i}\left[\Box+g_{2}^{2}(\varphi^{a})^{2}/2+{\rm gauge\ dependent}\right]c^{ab}\eta_{\mu\nu}
is the kinetic operator for the gauge field.
In what follows the sum over repeated spacetime indices is implied.
Taking the functional derivative of Eq. (
95
) with respect to the condensate gives

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\delta D_{A}[\varphi]}{\delta\varphi^{+}(x)}\right|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}=-\frac{g_{2}^{2}}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\varphi(x)\left.\Delta_{A}^{\mu\nu,++}(x,x;\varphi)\right|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}\,,

(96)

In the following, all two-point functions are to be understood as physical ones.

Now we expand the two-point function in insertions of the background field.
Care is needed since the zero-temperature mass of the gauge boson is zero when
\varphi=0
, we must understand what the perturbative parameter one is expanding in is.
A solution is provided by the non-zero thermal mass of the gauge boson
m_{A}=m_{A;\rm therm}
, which suggests the natural expansion parameter to be
g_{2}\varphi/m_{A}
. We then have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu\nu,ab}_{A}(x,y;\varphi)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{A}^{\mu\nu,ab}(x,y;0)

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,g_{2}^{2}\sum_{c}c\,\eta_{\alpha\beta}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\Delta_{A}^{\mu\alpha,ac}(x,x^{\prime};0)(\varphi^{c}(x^{\prime}))^{2}\Delta^{\beta\nu,cb}_{A}(x^{\prime},y;0)+\mathcal{O}((g_{2}\varphi)^{4})\,.

(97)

At this point, the two-point function
\Delta_{A}(x,y;0)
is the resummed propagator in the absence of the background field. This expansion is verified in Appendix
B
, and we relate it to the EoM of the two-point function. After expanding the CTP propagators in the mass of the gauge boson (
g_{2}\ll 1
), we can still expand it in terms of free thermal propagators (
g_{1}\ll 1
). We have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu\nu,ab}_{A}(x,y;0)=\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\nu,ab}(x,y)

\displaystyle+g_{1}^{2}{\rm tr_{s}}\sum_{cd}(cd)\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}\,\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\alpha,ac}(x,x_{1})S^{cd}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{1},x_{2})\gamma_{\beta}S^{dc}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{2},x_{1})\gamma_{\alpha}\Delta^{\beta\nu,db}_{A;\rm free}(x_{2},y)+\mathcal{O}(g^{4}_{1})\,,

(98)

where
{\rm tr}_{s}
is the trace in spinor space and
\Delta_{A,\rm free}^{\mu\nu,ab}(x,y)
is the gauge boson two-point function in absence of the fermionic loop corrections. Above, the minus sign from
(-\mathrm{i}g_{1})\times(-\mathrm{i}g_{1})
has been cancelled by the minus sign coming from the fermion loop:
\langle\bar{\psi}(x_{1})\psi(x_{1})\cdots\bar{\psi}(x_{2})\psi(x_{2})\rangle=-{\rm tr}\left(S_{\psi}(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots\ S_{\psi}(x_{2},x_{1}))\right)
.
This expansion is also verified in Appendix
B
. Substituting the expansion above into Eq. (
4.2.2
) generates a series of terms, which can be represented as Feynman diagrams, as follows
14
14

14

This is only to show schematically the expansion and we have ignored any numerical factors.

(99)

The first diagram in the second line is the free propagator in the absence of the background field, while all other diagrams are from higher-order terms in the expansion in
g^{2}_{1}
,
g^{2}_{2}
. All these diagrams are included when solving the Boltzmann equation for
f_{A}
.
The transition radiation contribution originates from the last diagram on the second line, containing one insertion of the condensate and one fermionic loop. At face value, its contribution is suppressed by additional powers of the couplings, however, we will see that its impact on the pressure is proportional to
\gamma_{w}
and thus can be dominant in the ultrarelativistic regime
[
88
]
. Note that there is another diagram in which the fermion loop is inserted into the gauge propagator in the lower half of the diagram, which contributes to a factor of two.
We write the last diagram in terms of free two-point functions

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu\nu,++}_{A}(x,x;\varphi)\supset\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{2}\sum_{cde}(cde){\rm tr}_{s}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}

\displaystyle\times\,\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\alpha,+c}(x,x^{\prime})\eta_{\alpha\beta}(\varphi^{c}(x^{\prime}))^{2}\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\beta\delta,cd}(x^{\prime},x_{1})S^{de}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{1},x_{2})\gamma_{\sigma}S^{ed}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{2},x_{1})\gamma_{\delta}\Delta^{\sigma\nu,e+}_{A;\rm free}(x_{2},x)

\displaystyle+(\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\,.

(100)

This translates, using Eq. (
96
), to

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{\delta D_{A}[\varphi]}{\delta\varphi^{+}(x)}\right|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}\supset\varphi(x)\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\Pi_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}^{\rm R}(x,x^{\prime})\varphi^{2}(x^{\prime})\,,

(101)

where

\displaystyle\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(x,x^{\prime})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{4}\sum_{cde}(cde)\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\alpha,+c}(x,x^{\prime})\eta_{\alpha\beta}{\rm tr}_{s}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\beta\delta,cd}(x^{\prime},x_{1})S^{de}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{1},x_{2})\gamma_{\sigma}S^{ed}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{2},x_{1})\gamma_{\delta}\Delta^{\sigma\nu,e+}_{A;\rm free}(x_{2},x)\,.

(102)

Here, the subscript “
\supset
TR’’ indicates that this induced self-energy contains the transition radiation contribution.
15
15

15

The usual definition of retarded self-energy is
\Pi^{\rm R}(x,x^{\prime})=\Pi^{++}(x,x^{\prime})-\Pi^{+-}(x,x^{\prime})=\sum_{c}(c)\Pi^{+c}(x,x^{\prime})
, where
c
is the Keldysh index on the vertex at
x^{\prime}
. Equation (
4.2.2
) also takes this form.

If one wishes to obtain Eq. (
101
) from the action principle, it corresponds to a contribution to the effective action
\Gamma
of the form

{\Gamma\supset}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ab}(ab)\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}\,[\varphi^{a}(x_{1})]^{2}\Pi^{ab}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(x_{1},x_{2})[\varphi^{b}(x_{2})]^{2}\sim\hbox to62.54pt{\vbox to39.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.68007pt\lower-16.89224pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Then applying
\delta\Gamma[\varphi^{a}]/\delta\varphi^{+}|_{\varphi^{+}=\varphi^{-}=\varphi}=0
would give us a term as Eq. (
101
). Here we note that the form of
\Pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(x,y)
is different from that of the self-energy defined through Eq. (
18
).

Following the procedure outlined in Section
3.3
, we can obtain the contribution to the friction as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\supset\rm TR}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}(z)}{\mathrm{d}z}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,\pi_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}^{\rm R}(z,z^{\prime})\varphi^{2}(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}q^{z}}{2\pi}q^{z}|\widetilde{\varphi^{2}}(q^{z})|^{2}\,{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}^{\rm R}(q^{z})\,,

(103)

where

\displaystyle\pi_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}^{\rm R}(z,z^{\prime})=\int\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\perp}\Pi_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}^{\rm R}(t-t^{\prime},\mathbf{x}_{\perp}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\perp};z,z^{\prime})\,.

(104)

Note that the free two-point functions both for the fermions and for the bosons are completely independent of the background field
\varphi
,
and in particular, enjoy full translational invariance, such that
{\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(z,z^{\prime})=\pi^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(z-z^{\prime})}
. If we take
g\varphi-gv_{b}
as a perturbation, then the gauge boson has a nonvanishing but
z
-independent mass. We observe that, differently from the pressure induced by pair production or by mixing, which are quadratic in the background field, the pressure due to transition radiation is quartic in
\varphi
. In particular, the Fourier transform of the square of the background field
\widetilde{\varphi^{2}}
appears.
As we did for the general
{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}_{\varphi}^{\rm R}
, to compute
{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}
it is simpler to use the relation

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\widetilde{\pi}^{\rm R}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\widetilde{\pi}^{>}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}-\widetilde{\pi}^{<}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}\right)\,,

(105)

where
\widetilde{\pi}^{\gtrless}_{\varphi;\rm TR}
correspond to

\displaystyle\Pi^{>}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(x,x^{\prime})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{4}\sum_{de}(de)\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\alpha,-+}(x,x^{\prime})\eta_{\alpha\beta}{\rm tr}_{s}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\beta\delta,+d}(x^{\prime},x_{1})S^{de}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{1},x_{2})\gamma_{\sigma}S^{ed}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{2},x_{1})\gamma_{\delta}\Delta^{\sigma\nu,e-}_{A;\rm free}(x_{2},x)\,,

(106a)

\displaystyle\Pi^{<}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(x,x^{\prime})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{4}\sum_{de}(de)\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\alpha,+-}(x,x^{\prime})\eta_{\alpha\beta}{\rm tr}_{s}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\beta\delta,-d}(x^{\prime},x_{1})S^{de}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{1},x_{2})\gamma_{\sigma}S^{ed}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{2},x_{1})\gamma_{\delta}\Delta^{\sigma\nu,e+}_{A;\rm free}(x_{2},x)\,.

(106b)

In terms of Feynman diagrams, these can be represented as

\displaystyle\Pi^{>}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}(x,x^{\prime})\quad\sim\quad\sum_{d,e}\hbox to83.15pt{\vbox to39.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.50212pt\lower-16.89224pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

(107b)

where we have also indicated the Keldysh indices. Each function is a sum of four terms. Different choices of
\{d,e\}
give different cuttings of the diagram and thus different particle processes. Since in the transition radiation process, both fermions are on-shell external states, and since it is the fermion Wightman functions
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\psi}^{<}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\psi}^{>}
that enforce on-shell conditions by being proportional to
{\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})}
(see Eqs. (
92
)), and furthermore carry the required statistical factors of
f_{\psi}
and
1-f_{\psi}
, the relevant term we are tracing should have
d
and
e
taking different signs.

Our formula for the transition radiation pressure amounts to a leading-order term in the expansion in
g_{2}^{2}\varphi^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}/4
of the following diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+...\,.

(109)

To be more specific, the transition radiation process is represented by

(110)

where we see an internal, off-shell gauge propagator.
This means that the terms in
\Pi^{>}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}
and
\Pi^{<}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}
that give rise to the transition radiation process should have
d
and
e
taking the same sign as their nearest neighbour gauge mass vertex (
g_{2}^{2}\varphi^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}
) so that we would have the internal gauge propagator. Therefore we choose the
\{d=+,e=-\}
term from
\Pi^{>}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}
, and the
\{d=-,e=+\}
term from
\Pi^{<}_{\varphi;\supset\rm TR}
. In conclusion, we have identified the relevant terms that contribute to the transition radiation process:

\displaystyle\ ={\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\eta_{\mu\nu}g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{4}\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\mu\alpha,>}(x,x^{\prime})\eta_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\times{\rm tr}_{s}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{1}\mathrm{d}^{4}x_{2}\Delta_{A;\rm free}^{\beta\delta,++}(x^{\prime},x_{1})S^{<}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{1},x_{2})\gamma_{\sigma}S^{>}_{\psi;\rm free}(x_{2},x_{1})\gamma_{\delta}\Delta^{\sigma\nu,--}_{A;\rm free}(x_{2},x)\,,

(111a)

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.75938pt}{-2.0228pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
